- **32** "A maskil" is a word of uncertain meaning, perhaps "a teaching" or "a skilful psalm". It is also in the headings of Psalms 42, 44 and others. Psalm 32 is one of the seven penitential psalms of later tradition (see also 6, 38, 51, 102, 130 and 143).
- **32:1** "Blessed" is Hebrew *ashrei*, as in 1:1. The three words for wrongdoing (*pesha*, *chata'ah*, *avon*) are matched by three for forgiveness ("forgiven", "covered" and "counts no"). The verses 1 and 2 are quoted in Romans 4:7-8.
- **32:2** "Counts no iniquity" translates *lo yachshov avon*. "Spirit" is *ruach*. See entry H-RUACH.
- **32:3** "When I kept silent" refers to the time before confession.
- **32:4** "Your hand was heavy upon me" is a figure of God's pressure (see Job 23:2). "My strength was dried up as by the heat of summer" translates *nehpakh leshadi be-charvonei qayits*, which is hard and may be read "my sap was turned to the drought of summer".
- **32:5** "I acknowledged", "I did not cover" and "I will confess" are three verbs for confession. "You forgave the guilt of my sin" translates *ve-attah nasata avon chatta'ti*.
- **32:6** "Everyone who is godly" is *kol chasid*. See entry H-HESED. "At a time when you may be found" translates *le-et metso*, which is hard and may be read "at a time of finding".
- **32:7** "A hiding place" translates *seter*. "Shouts of deliverance" is *ronnei palet*.
- **32:8** "I will instruct you" begins a speech. It is not clear whether the speaker is God or the poet. "I will counsel you with my eye upon you" translates *a'atsah alekha eini*, which is hard.
- **32:9** "Bit and bridle" translates *metheg va-resen*. "Or it will not stay near you" is hard in the Hebrew (*bal qarov elekha*) and may be read "that they may not come near you".
- **32:10** "Steadfast love" is *hesed*. See entry H-HESED.
- **32:11** "Upright in heart" ends the psalm with the praise of the righteous.
- **33** Psalm 33 has no heading in the Hebrew. The Greek calls it a psalm of David. It follows on from 32:11, with "rejoice in the LORD, you righteous". The numbering is the same in Hebrew and English. In Psalm 34 the heading is verse 1, so that English 34:1-22 is Hebrew 34:2-23. Psalm 35 is numbered alike. Psalms 36, 38, 39, 40 and 41 each have the heading as verse 1, so that the Hebrew verses are one higher (English 36:1-12 is Hebrew 36:2-13, and so on). Psalm 37 is numbered alike. In the Greek and Latin numbering these psalms are one lower, from 32 to 40. The psalm has twenty-two verses, the number of letters of the alphabet, but it is not an acrostic.
- **33:2** "The lyre" is Hebrew *kinnor* and "the harp of ten strings" is *nevel asor*.
- **33:3** "A new song" is *shir chadash*, as in 40:3, 96:1, 98:1, 144:9 and 149:1. "Loud shouts" is *teru'ah*.
- **33:5** "Righteousness" is *tsedaqah*. See entry H-TSEDAQAH. "Justice" is *mishpat*. "The steadfast love of the LORD" is *chesed YHWH*. See entry H-HESED.
- **33:6** "By the word of the LORD the heavens were made" refers to creation by speech (Genesis 1; see also Hebrews 11:3). "The breath of his mouth" is *ruach pivv*. See entry H-RUACH. "All their host" is the stars.
- **33:7** "He gathers the waters of the sea as in a heap" translates *ke-ned*, as in Exodus 15:8.
- **33:10** "The LORD brings the counsel of the nations to nothing" is contrasted with "the counsel of the LORD" in verse 11.
- **33:15** "He who fashions the hearts of them all" translates *ha-yotser yachad libbam*, which may also be read "who fashions all their hearts alike".
- **33:17** "A false hope" is Hebrew *sheqer*. The war horse is not what saves (see 20:7).
- **33:19** "Their soul" is *nafsham*. See entry H-NEPHESH.
- **33:21** "His holy name" is *shem qodsho*. See entry H-QADOSH.
- **34** "Abimelech" is the title of the Philistine kings of Gath. The story is in 1 Samuel 21:10-15, where the king is called Achish. "Changed his behaviour" refers to David's pretending to be mad. Psalm 34 is an acrostic, in which the lines begin with the Hebrew alphabet in turn. The letter *vav* is missing, and an extra verse (22) begins with the letter *pe*, as in Psalm 25. The WABV text does not try to reproduce it.
- **34:2** "My soul makes its boast in the LORD" uses *nafshi*. See entry H-NEPHESH. "The humble" is *anavim*.
- **34:5** "Those who look to him are radiant" translates *hibbitu elav ve-naharu*, which may also be read "they looked to him and were radiant" or "flowed to him".
- **34:7** "The angel of the LORD encamps around those who fear him" is a figure of protection.
- **34:8** "Taste and see that the LORD is good" is quoted in 1 Peter 2:3.
- **34:9** "His holy ones" is *qedoshav*. See entry H-QADOSH.
- **34:10** "The young lions" is *kefirim*. The Greek has "the rich".
- **34:14** "Peace" is *shalom*. See entry H-SHALOM. The verses 12 to 16 are quoted in 1 Peter 3:10-12.
- **34:18** "The crushed in spirit" uses *ruach*. See entry H-RUACH.
- **34:20** "He keeps all his bones. Not one of them is broken" is quoted in John 19:36, together with Exodus 12:46.
- **34:21** "Evil will kill the wicked" translates *temotet rasha ra'ah*. "Will be condemned" is *ye'shamu*, "will bear guilt".
- **34:22** "The LORD redeems the soul of his servants" is the extra verse that begins with *pe*, outside the acrostic.
- **35:1** "Contend" and "fight" translate *riv* and *lacham*, the language of the court and of the battle.
- **35:3** "Draw the spear and javelin" translates *ha-rek chanit u-segor*. The word *segor* may mean "javelin" or "close off". "Say to my soul, 'I am your salvation'" asks for God's own word.
- **35:4** The prayers of verses 4 to 8 and 26 are rendered as written. They ask that those who seek the poet's life be shamed and caught.
- **35:5** "The angel of the LORD driving them away" is a figure of the LORD's messenger.
- **35:7** "Without cause" is Hebrew *chinnam*, as in Job 1:9.
- **35:11** "Malicious witnesses" is *edei chamas*, "witnesses of violence".
- **35:12** "My soul is bereft" translates *shekhol le-nafshi*, "bereavement to my soul".
- **35:13** "I prayed with my head bowed on my chest" translates *u-tefillati al-cheqi tashuv*, literally "my prayer returned to my bosom". It may mean that he prayed with a bowed head, or that his prayer returned unanswered.
- **35:15** "Strangers whom I did not know" translates *nekhim*, a rare word which may mean "wretches" or "smiters".
- **35:16** "Like godless mockers at a feast" translates a very hard phrase (*be-chanfei la'agei ma'og*), literally "with the profane mockers of a cake". The sense is uncertain.
- **35:17** "My only life" translates *yechidati*. "The lions" is *kefirim*.
- **35:19** "Wink the eye" is a sign of malice.
- **35:20** "Those who are quiet in the land" translates *rig'ei erets*.
- **35:24** "According to your righteousness" is *ke-tsidqekha*. See entry H-TSEDAQAH.
- **35:27** "The welfare of his servant" translates *shelom avdo*, "the peace of his servant".
- **36:1** "Transgression speaks to the wicked deep in his heart" follows the Greek, the Syriac and many manuscripts, which have "his heart". The Hebrew text has "my heart" (*be-qerev libbi*), which may mean that the poet hears the voice of transgression within himself. The word *ne'um* ("speaks") is the word for a divine oracle, so that transgression speaks like a false prophet. The WABV text departs from the Hebrew reading and is flagged for the scholar. "The servant of the LORD" is a title of David (see 18).
- **36:2** "That his iniquity will not be found out and hated" translates a hard Hebrew phrase (*li-mtso avono li-sno*).
- **36:5** "Your steadfast love" is *chasdekha*. See entry H-HESED. "Your faithfulness" is *emunatekha*.
- **36:6** "Your righteousness is like the mountains of God" translates *tsidqatekha ke-harerei El*. See entry H-TSEDAQAH. "The mountains of God" are the greatest mountains. "The great deep" is *tehom rabbah*.
- **36:7** "How precious" is Hebrew *mah yaqar*. "The shadow of your wings" is as in 17:8.
- **36:8** "The river of your delights" translates *nachal adanekha*, which echoes the name Eden (see Genesis 2:10).
- **36:9** "The fountain of life" is *meqor chayyim*. "In your light we see light" says that God is the source of all knowledge.
- **36:12** "There the evildoers lie fallen" points to the place of their fall. The Hebrew *sham* means "there".
- **37** Psalm 37 is an acrostic, in which the lines begin with the letters of the Hebrew alphabet in turn, a letter to every two lines or so. The WABV text does not try to reproduce it. The psalm is a wisdom psalm. Its theme is that the wicked will not last and that the righteous will inherit the land.
- **37:1** "Do not fret" translates *al-tithar*, "do not burn". It is repeated in verses 7 and 8.
- **37:3** "Keep faith" translates *re'eh emunah*, which may mean "feed on faithfulness", "befriend faithfulness" or "pasture on faithfulness".
- **37:4** "Delight yourself in the LORD" translates *hit'anag*.
- **37:5** "Commit your way to the LORD" translates *gol al-YHWH darkekha*, literally "roll your way on the LORD".
- **37:6** "Your righteousness" is *tsidqekha*. See entry H-TSEDAQAH.
- **37:7** "Be still before the LORD" translates *dom*, "be silent". "Wait patiently" is *hitcholel*.
- **37:9** "Will inherit the land" is the theme of verses 9, 11, 22, 29 and 34. Verse 11 is quoted in Matthew 5:5.
- **37:11** "The meek" is *anavim*. "Peace" is *shalom*. See entry H-SHALOM.
- **37:13** "The Lord laughs at the wicked" uses *Adonai* (see 2:4).
- **37:20** "Like the beauty of the pastures" translates *ke-yiqar karim*, which may also be read "like the fat of lambs". The sense is that they vanish in smoke.
- **37:25** "I have been young, and now I am old, yet I have not seen the righteous forsaken" is the poet's own experience. It is a general observation and not a rule without exception, and the book of Job is its counterpart.
- **37:28** "His faithful ones" is Hebrew *chasidav*. See entry H-HESED.
- **37:31** "The teaching of his God" is *torat Elohav*. See entry H-TORAH.
- **37:35** "Like a green tree in its native soil" translates *ke-ezrach ra'anan*, in which *ezrach* means "native" or "a native tree" and may be a type of tree. The Greek has "like the cedars of Lebanon".
- **37:37** "A future for the person of peace" translates *acharit le-ish shalom*. The word *acharit* may mean "future", "end" or "posterity".
- **38** "For the memorial offering" translates *le-hazkir*, "to bring to remembrance", a heading also used in Psalm 70. The psalm is one of the seven penitential psalms of later tradition.
- **38:1** "LORD, do not rebuke me in your anger" is the same as 6:1.
- **38:2** "Your arrows" and "your hand" are figures of God's blows (see Job 6:4).
- **38:3** "Soundness" translates *metom* and "indignation" is *za'am*.
- **38:5** "My wounds stink and fester" translates *hivshu nammaqu chabburotai*. "My foolishness" is *ivvalti*.
- **38:6** "Bent" translates *na'avetti*.
- **38:7** "My loins are full of burning" translates *kesalai male'u niqleh*, in which *niqleh* may mean "burning" or "shameful".
- **38:8** "Feeble" translates *nefugoti*, "numb".
- **38:10** "My heart throbs" translates the rare word *sechartsar*.
- **38:11** "My plague" is Hebrew *nega*, a word used of skin disease in Leviticus 13.
- **38:13** "Like a deaf man who does not hear" says that the poet does not answer his accusers.
- **38:15** "It is you ... who will answer" is the hope of the psalm.
- **38:17** "Ready to fall" translates *le-tsela*, "to limp".
- **38:18** "I confess my iniquity" is the one open confession in the psalm. "I am sorry" translates *ede'ag*, "I am anxious".
- **38:19** "My foes are vigorous" translates *oyevai chayyim atsemu*, which may also be read "my enemies without cause are strong" (*chinnam*).
- **38:20** "Those who repay me evil for good" is as in 35:12.
- **39** "Jeduthun" was one of the Levite leaders of the temple music (see 1 Chronicles 16:41 and 25:1). The heading may name a choir or a tune.
- **39:1** "I will guard my ways" and "a muzzle" describe the poet's attempt to keep silent in the presence of the wicked.
- **39:2** "I held my peace to no avail" translates *he'echashti mi-tov*, literally "I was silent from good", which may mean "even from good words" or "to no good".
- **39:3** "As I mused, the fire burned" is a figure of a thought that must come out.
- **39:4** "Make me know my end" translates *hodi'eni YHWH qitsi*. "How fleeting I am" translates *chadel*, "how transient".
- **39:5** "A mere breath" is Hebrew *hevel*, the word of Ecclesiastes. "Selah" is explained at Psalm 3:2.
- **39:6** "A shadow" translates *tselem*, which may also mean "an image". "A person heaps up wealth and does not know who will gather it" is a saying like Ecclesiastes 2:18-19.
- **39:8** "The fool" is *naval*, as in 14:1.
- **39:9** "I am mute ... for it is you who have done it" says that the poet's suffering is from God.
- **39:10** "Your stroke" is *nig'ekha*. "The hostility of your hand" translates *mi-tigrat yadekha*.
- **39:11** "Like a moth what is dear to him" refers to something precious that is eaten away.
- **39:12** "A stranger with you, a guest, like all my ancestors" translates *ger immakh toshav* (see Leviticus 25:23, 1 Chronicles 29:15 and Hebrews 11:13).
- **39:13** "Look away from me, that I may smile again" translates *sha'eh mimmenni ve-avligah*, which may be read "that I may be cheerful". It is a very bold prayer, that God would stop watching him.
- **40** The Hebrew heading is verse 1, so that English 40:1-17 is Hebrew 40:2-18.
- **40:1** "I waited patiently" translates *qavoh qiviti*, literally "waiting, I waited".
- **40:2** "The pit of destruction" translates *bor sha'on*, which may also mean "the pit of noise". "The miry bog" is *tit ha-yaven*.
- **40:3** "A new song" is as in 33:3.
- **40:4** "The proud" translates *rehavim*, a rare word that may mean "the arrogant" or may be the name Rahab (see 87:4 and 89:10). "Those who go astray after a lie" says that they follow what is false.
- **40:5** "No one can compare with you" translates *ein arokh elekha*, which is hard and may also be read "no one can be set in order before you".
- **40:6** "You have given me an open ear" translates *oznayim karita li*, literally "ears you have dug for me". The Greek has "a body you have prepared for me", which is quoted in Hebrews 10:5. The WABV text follows the Hebrew. "In sacrifice and offering you have not delighted" is like 50:8-15 and 51:16-17.
- **40:7** "In the scroll of the book it is written of me" translates *be-megillat sefer katuv alai*, which may mean "written about me" or "prescribed for me". It may refer to the law of the king (Deuteronomy 17:18-20). The words are quoted in Hebrews 10:7.
- **40:8** "Your teaching" is *toratekha*. See entry H-TORAH. "Is within my heart" translates *be-toch me'ai*, "in my inward parts".
- **40:9** "The glad news of deliverance" translates *bissarti tsedeq*, in which *tsedeq* means "righteousness" or "deliverance". See entry H-TSEDAQAH.
- **40:10** "Your steadfast love" is *chasdekha*. See entry H-HESED. "Your faithfulness" is *amittekha*.
- **40:12** "I cannot see" translates *lo yakholti lir'ot*. "They are more than the hairs of my head" is a figure of numbers (see 69:4).
- **40:13** The verses 13 to 17 are nearly the same as Psalm 70. The differences are small: in Psalm 70 the divine name is *Elohim* in verse 1, and some words differ.
- **40:14** "Who seek to snatch away my life" is a prayer against the enemies, rendered as written.
- **40:15** "Appalled" translates *yashommu*, "be desolate". "Aha, aha" is a cry of mockery (see 35:21).
- **40:17** "The Lord takes thought for me" translates *Adonai yachshav li*.
- **41:1** "Blessed is the one who considers the poor" translates *ashrei maskil el-dal*. The poor are the weak and the sick. "The day of trouble" is *yom ra'ah*.
- **41:2** "You do not give him up to the will of his enemies" follows the Greek and the reading tradition ("do not give him up"). The written text has "you do not give him".
- **41:3** "The LORD sustains him on his sickbed" translates *eres devai*. "You restore him to full health" translates *kol mishkavo hafakhta*, literally "you turn all his bed", which is hard.
- **41:4** "Heal me, for I have sinned against you" joins sickness with sin (see 38:3).
- **41:6** "Empty words" is Hebrew *shav*.
- **41:8** "A deadly thing is poured out on him" translates *devar-beliya'al yatsuq bo*, in which *devar beliya'al* may mean "a deadly thing", "a word of wickedness" or "a thing of destruction".
- **41:9** "My close friend" translates *ish shelomi*, "the man of my peace". "Who ate my bread, has lifted his heel against me" is quoted in John 13:18. "Lifted his heel" is a figure of treachery.
- **41:10** "That I may repay them" translates *va-ashallemah lahem*, "that I may requite them". The line is rendered as written.
- **41:12** "Because of my integrity" is Hebrew *be-tummi*. "Set me in your presence for ever" ends the psalm.
- **41:13** "Blessed be the LORD, the God of Israel, from everlasting to everlasting. Amen and Amen" is the closing blessing of Book One. It is not part of the psalm. The other books end in the same way (72:18-19; 89:52; 106:48).
- **42** Book Two (Psalms 42 to 72) begins here. In Psalms 42 to 83 the divine name in the Hebrew is mostly *Elohim* ("God") and less often *YHWH* ("the LORD"), and this has been kept. In Psalms 42, 44, 45, 46, 47, 48 and 49 the Hebrew heading is verse 1, so that English 42:1-11 is Hebrew 42:2-12, English 44:1-26 is Hebrew 44:2-27, English 45:1-17 is Hebrew 45:2-18, English 46:1-11 is Hebrew 46:2-12, English 47:1-9 is Hebrew 47:2-10, English 48:1-14 is Hebrew 48:2-15, and English 49:1-20 is Hebrew 49:2-21. Psalm 43 is numbered alike. In the Greek and Latin numbering these psalms are one lower, from 41 to 48. "The sons of Korah" were a family of temple singers (see Numbers 26:11 and 1 Chronicles 6:31-37).
- **42:1** "As a deer pants for flowing streams" uses a feminine verb (*ta'arog*), so that it may be "a doe". "Flowing streams" translates *afiqei mayim*.
- **42:2** "Appear before God" translates the verb *era'eh*, which may be read "see the face of God".
- **42:3** "Where is your God?" is the mockery of the enemies. It is repeated in verse 10.
- **42:4** "I would go with the crowd and lead them in procession" translates two rare words (*essakh* and *eddaddem*), whose meaning is uncertain. "A multitude keeping festival" is *hamon chogeg*.
- **42:5** "Why are you cast down, my soul" begins the refrain, which is repeated in 42:11 and 43:5. "My salvation" follows the refrain in 42:11 and 43:5 and the Greek and Syriac. The Hebrew in 42:5 has "the salvations of his face", and the verse division then runs through the line. The WABV text departs from the Hebrew reading in this verse and is flagged for the scholar.
- **42:6** "Mount Mizar" means "little mountain". The poet is in the far north, near the sources of the Jordan.
- **42:7** "Deep calls to deep" translates *tehom el tehom*. "Your waterfalls" translates *tsinnorekha*, which may mean "channels" or "waterspouts".
- **42:8** "The LORD commands his steadfast love" is the only place in Psalms 42 and 43 where the divine name *YHWH* is used. "His steadfast love" is *chasdo*. See entry H-HESED.
- **42:10** "As with a deadly wound in my bones" translates *be-retsach be-atsmotai*, literally "in a shattering in my bones".
- **43:1** "Vindicate me" and "defend my cause" are the verbs *shofeteni* and *rivah riveni*. "An ungodly people" translates *goy lo chasid*, "a nation not faithful". See entry H-HESED.
- **43:3** "Your light and your truth" are spoken of as persons who lead. "Your holy hill" is *har qodshekha*. See entry H-QADOSH. "Your dwelling" is *mishkenotekha*.
- **43:4** "God my exceeding joy" translates *El simchat gili*, "God, the joy of my gladness".
- **44** "Maskil" is explained at Psalm 32.
- **44:1** "Our ancestors have told us" refers to the telling of the story of the exodus and the conquest (see Exodus 12:26-27 and Psalm 78:3-4).
- **44:2** "You with your own hand drove out the nations, but them you planted" refers to the conquest of the land (see Exodus 15:17).
- **44:3** "The light of your face" is God's favour.
- **44:4** "You are my King, God" follows the Hebrew. The Greek has "you are my King and my God". "Order salvation" translates *tsavveh yeshu'ot*.
- **44:9** "But you have rejected us" begins the complaint (verses 9 to 16). It is not a confession.
- **44:12** "You have sold your people for a trifle, asking no high price for them" translates *te-mkor amekha be-lo-hon ve-lo rivvachta bi-mchireihem*. The second half is hard and may be read "you did not profit by their price".
- **44:14** "A shaking of the head" translates *menod rosh*, a gesture of contempt.
- **44:17** "We have not been false to your covenant" uses *berit*. See entry H-BERIT. The people claim that they have kept faith, and the claim is rendered as written, although it sets the psalm against the view of the history of Israel in Deuteronomy 28.
- **44:19** "In the place of jackals" translates *be-meqom tannim*, which may mean "in the place of jackals" or "in the place of dragons". "Deep darkness" translates *tsalmavet*.
- **44:21** "He knows the secrets of the heart" is the people's appeal to God's knowledge.
- **44:22** "Yet for your sake we are killed all day long" is quoted in Romans 8:36.
- **44:23** "Wake up! Why are you sleeping, Lord?" is rendered as written. It is a bold prayer, and it uses *Adonai*.
- **44:25** "Our soul" is *nafshenu*. See entry H-NEPHESH.
- **44:26** "For the sake of your steadfast love" translates *le-ma'an chasdekha*. See entry H-HESED.
- **45** "According to Lilies" (*al shoshannim*) names a tune. "A love song" translates *shir yedidot*, literally "a song of loves".
- **45:1** "A pleasing theme" is *davar tov*. "A skilful scribe" is *sofer mahir*.
- **45:2** "The most handsome" translates the doubled form *yafyafita*. "Therefore God has blessed you for ever" gives the ground for the praise.
- **45:3** "Your splendour and majesty" is *hodekha ve-hadarekha*.
- **45:4** "For the cause of truth and humility and righteousness" translates *al-devar emet ve-anvah-tsedeq*, which is hard. See entry H-TSEDAQAH.
- **45:6** "Your throne, God, is for ever and ever" is hard in the Hebrew (*kis'akha Elohim olam va-ed*). It may be read "Your throne, O God" (the king is addressed as *elohim*, as one who acts for God), or "Your throne is God's, for ever", or "Your divine throne". The verses 6 and 7 are quoted in Hebrews 1:8-9.
- **45:7** "God, your God, has anointed you" translates *meshachekha Elohim Elohekha*. The verb *mashach* means "anoint" (see entry H-MASHIACH).
- **45:8** "Stringed instruments" translates *minni*, which may also be read as the name of a people (see Jeremiah 51:27). "Ivory palaces" refers to rooms with ivory inlay.
- **45:9** "The queen" translates *shegal*, a rare word for the royal consort. "The gold of Ophir" is fine gold (see 1 Kings 9:28).
- **45:11** "He is your lord" translates *hu adonayikh*. The verses 10 and 11 are addressed to the bride.
- **45:12** "The people of Tyre" translates *bat-tsor*, "the daughter of Tyre".
- **45:13** "All glorious is the princess in her chamber" translates *kol kevudah bat-melekh penimah*, which may mean "inside" or "within the palace".
- **45:16** "In place of your fathers will be your sons" is a promise of descendants.
- **45:17** "I will make your name remembered" is the poet's promise to the king.
- **46** "According to Alamoth" (*al alamot*) is a musical term. *Alamot* means "young women" and may refer to a high voice (see 1 Chronicles 15:20).
- **46:1** "A very present help in trouble" translates *ezrah be-tsarot nimtsa me'od*, literally "a help in troubles, found very much".
- **46:2** "The earth gives way" and "the mountains are moved into the heart of the sea" are figures of the end of order. The sea is the force of chaos.
- **46:4** "There is a river whose streams make glad the city of God" is a figure. Jerusalem has no river, and the poet is using the image of the waters of Eden (see Genesis 2:10, Ezekiel 47 and Isaiah 33:21). "The holy dwelling" translates *qedosh mishkenei*. See entry H-QADOSH. "The Most High" is *Elyon*.
- **46:5** "When morning dawns" translates *li-fnot boqer*, literally "at the turning of the morning".
- **46:7** "The LORD of hosts is with us" is a refrain, which is also verse 11. It may once have followed verse 3, where a space is left in the Hebrew. "With us" is *immanu* (see Isaiah 7:14).
- **46:8** "Desolations" translates *shammot*, which may also be read "wonders".
- **46:9** "He burns the chariots with fire" is a figure of the end of war.
- **46:10** "Be still" translates *harpu*, "let go" or "stop". It is spoken to the nations or to the people and is not a call for quiet prayer. "I will be exalted among the nations" says that God will be known by all.
- **47:1** "Clap your hands" is a sign of acclaiming a king.
- **47:2** "The Most High" is *Elyon*. "A great king over all the earth" is the theme of the enthronement psalms (see 93, 96 to 99).
- **47:4** "The pride of Jacob" translates *ge'on Ya'aqov*.
- **47:5** "God has gone up" refers to the enthronement of God, which may have been shown by the ark going up to the temple. "The sound of a trumpet" is the *shofar*.
- **47:7** "A psalm" translates *maskil*, so that the line may read "sing praises with understanding".
- **47:8** "God reigns over the nations" is *malakh Elohim al-goyim*. "His holy throne" is *kisse qodsho*. See entry H-QADOSH.
- **47:9** "The princes of the peoples gather as the people of the God of Abraham" translates *am Elohei Avraham*, which may also be read "with the people of the God of Abraham". "The shields of the earth" are the rulers.
- **48:1** "His holy mountain" is *har qodsho*. See entry H-QADOSH.
- **48:2** "In the far north" translates *yarketei tsafon*. *Tsafon* (Zaphon) is also the name of the mountain of the gods in the stories of Canaan, so that the poet gives the name to Zion. "Beautiful in elevation" translates *yefeh nof*.
- **48:3** "Citadels" translates *armenot*. "A fortress" is *misgav*.
- **48:4** "The kings assembled" refers to an attack on Jerusalem that failed (see 46:6 and Isaiah 36 and 37).
- **48:6** "Anguish like that of a woman in labour" is a figure of terror.
- **48:7** "By the east wind you shattered the ships of Tarshish" refers to a storm at sea, a figure of the power of God.
- **48:9** "Your steadfast love" is *chasdekha*. See entry H-HESED. "In the midst of your temple" translates *be-qerev heikhalekha*.
- **48:10** "Righteousness" is *tsedeq*. See entry H-TSEDAQAH.
- **48:11** "The daughters of Judah" are the towns of Judah.
- **48:14** "He will guide us for ever" translates *hu yenahagenu al-mut*, in which *al-mut* may mean "for ever", "even to death" or "to Muth", and so may be a musical term (see the heading of Psalm 9).
- **49:1** "All inhabitants of the world" translates *kol yoshvei cheled*.
- **49:3** "Wisdom" and "understanding" are both plural in the Hebrew (*chokhmot* and *tevunot*).
- **49:4** "A proverb" is *mashal* and "my riddle" is *chidati*.
- **49:5** "When the iniquity of my persecutors surrounds me" translates *avon aqevai yesubbeni*, literally "the iniquity of my heels surrounds me", which is hard. It may mean the iniquity of those who pursue or the poet's own sins.
- **49:7** "Truly no one can ransom another" translates *ach pado lo-yifdeh ish*, in which *ach* may mean "truly" or "a brother", so that the line may read "no brother can ransom". "The price of his life" is *kofro*. See entry H-KAPPER.
- **49:8** "Can never suffice" translates *va-chadal le-olam*, "and it ceases for ever".
- **49:9** "The pit" is *shachat*.
- **49:11** "Their graves are their homes for ever" follows the Greek and the Syriac (*qivram*). The Hebrew has *qirbam*, "their inward thought is that their houses will last for ever". The WABV text departs from the Hebrew reading and is flagged for the scholar.
- **49:12** "A person in his pomp does not remain" translates *adam bi-yeqar bal yalin*. The refrain is repeated in verse 20, where the Hebrew has "has no understanding" (*lo yavin*).
- **49:13** "Foolish confidence" translates *kesel lamo*. "People approve of their boasts" translates *be-fihem yirtsu*, literally "approve their mouths".
- **49:14** "Death will be their shepherd" translates *mavet yir'em*, in which the verb may mean "shepherd" or "feed on". "The upright will rule over them in the morning" is hard in the Hebrew. "Their form will be consumed in Sheol, with no place to dwell" is also hard (*ve-tsuram le-vallot Sheol mi-zevul lo*). See entry H-SHEOL.
- **49:15** "God will ransom my soul from the power of Sheol, for he will receive me" translates *Elohim yifdeh nafshi mi-yad Sheol ki yiqachani*. See entry H-NEPHESH. The verb *laqach* "receive" or "take" is used of Enoch (Genesis 5:24) and Elijah (2 Kings 2:3), and see 73:24. The poet may hope for rescue from an early death or for life with God beyond death. The Hebrew does not say which.
- **49:18** "Though in his lifetime he counts himself blessed" is hard in the Hebrew, and the line may be read as the person's own thought.
- **49:19** "His soul will go to the generation of his fathers, who will never again see light" refers to the dead, to whom the light of life does not come again.
- **50** In Psalm 50 the heading is part of verse 1, and the numbering is the same in Hebrew and English. In Psalms 51, 52 and 54 the Hebrew heading is verses 1 and 2, so that English 51:1-19 is Hebrew 51:3-21, English 52:1-9 is Hebrew 52:3-11, and English 54:1-7 is Hebrew 54:3-9. In Psalms 53, 55, 56, 57, 58 and 59 the heading is verse 1, so that English 53:1-6 is Hebrew 53:2-7, English 55:1-23 is Hebrew 55:2-24, English 56:1-13 is Hebrew 56:2-14, English 57:1-11 is Hebrew 57:2-12, English 58:1-11 is Hebrew 58:2-12, and English 59:1-17 is Hebrew 59:2-18. In the Greek and Latin numbering these psalms are one lower, from 49 to 58. "Asaph" was a Levite leader of the temple music (see 1 Chronicles 6:39, 15:17 and 2 Chronicles 29:30). Psalms 50 and 73 to 83 are headed with his name.
- **50:1** "The Mighty One, God, the LORD" translates *El Elohim YHWH*, three names of God together. "Summons the earth" says that God calls all the earth to court.
- **50:2** "The perfection of beauty" translates *mikhlal yofi*. "God shines forth" translates *hofia*.
- **50:3** "A devouring fire" and "a mighty tempest" recall the appearance of God at Sinai (see Exodus 19:16-19).
- **50:5** "My faithful ones" translates *chasidai*. See entry H-HESED. "A covenant with me by sacrifice" refers to the covenant at Sinai (Exodus 24:5-8). See entry H-BERIT.
- **50:6** "His righteousness" is *tsidqo*. See entry H-TSEDAQAH.
- **50:8** "I do not rebuke you for your sacrifices" may also be read "I do not rebuke you because of your sacrifices". The charge is not that the offerings are missing but that they are wrongly understood.
- **50:12** "If I were hungry, I would not tell you" says that God does not need to be fed (see 1 Samuel 15:22 and Isaiah 1:11).
- **50:14** "A sacrifice of thanksgiving" is *zevach toda*. "The Most High" is *Elyon*.
- **50:16** "To the wicked God says" begins the second charge (verses 16 to 21). The wicked recite the law but do not keep it.
- **50:17** "Discipline" is Hebrew *musar*, "instruction".
- **50:18** "A thief" and "adulterers" recall the commandments (Exodus 20:14-15).
- **50:21** "You thought that I was just like you" translates *hayot ehyeh kamokha*, which is hard and may be read "you thought that I would be like you".
- **50:22** "I will tear you apart" translates *etrof*, as of a beast tearing its prey.
- **50:23** "To the one who orders his way rightly" translates *ve-sam derekh*, which is hard. The Greek has a different text.
- **51** The Hebrew heading is verses 1 and 2. The story of Nathan's rebuke of David is in 2 Samuel 12:1-15, and of the sin with Bathsheba in 2 Samuel 11. The psalm is one of the seven penitential psalms of later tradition. The three words for wrongdoing are *pesha* ("transgression"), *avon* ("iniquity") and *chatta'ah* ("sin"), and the three verbs of cleansing are "blot out", "wash" and "cleanse".
- **51:1** "Your steadfast love" is *chasdekha*. See entry H-HESED. "Abundant compassion" translates *rov rachamekha*.
- **51:2** "Wash me thoroughly" translates *harbeh kabbesni*, the verb for washing cloth.
- **51:4** "Against you, you only, have I sinned" translates *lekha levaddekha chatati*. The sin has been done to other people as well, and the psalm says that all sin is against God. "So that you are justified in your sentence and blameless in your judgement" is quoted in Romans 3:4, where the Greek has "when you judge".
- **51:5** "I was brought forth in iniquity, and in sin my mother conceived me" is rendered as written, with no explanation. The verse speaks of the poet's condition from his birth. Its meaning has been debated for a long time, and the Hebrew does not say whose sin is meant, or whether the verse speaks of a sinful state or of the setting of his birth. The WABV text does not choose.
- **51:6** "The inward being" translates *tuchot*, a rare word (see Job 38:36). "The secret heart" translates *satum*, "the hidden place".
- **51:7** "Purge me with hyssop" translates *techatte'eni be-ezov*, in which the verb is formed from *chet*, "sin", and means "un-sin me". Hyssop was used in rites of cleansing (see Leviticus 14:4 and Numbers 19:18).
- **51:8** "The bones that you have broken" says that the poet's grief is a crushing by God.
- **51:10** "Create in me a clean heart" uses the verb *bara*, which is used of God's creation. "A steadfast spirit" translates *ruach nakhon*. See entry H-RUACH.
- **51:11** "Your holy spirit" translates *ruach qodshekha*. The phrase is found in the Old Testament only here and in Isaiah 63:10-11. See entry H-QADOSH.
- **51:12** "A willing spirit" translates *ruach nedivah*, "a generous spirit".
- **51:13** "Will return to you" uses *shuv*. See entry H-SHUV.
- **51:14** "Bloodguilt" translates *damim*, literally "bloods". It refers to the guilt of a death (see 2 Samuel 11:14-17). "Your righteousness" is *tsidqatekha*. See entry H-TSEDAQAH.
- **51:15** "Lord" is *Adonai*.
- **51:17** "A broken spirit" translates *ruach nishbarah* and "a broken and contrite heart" is *lev nishbar ve-nidkeh*. The verse is like 40:6 and 50:14.
- **51:18** The last two verses (18 and 19) speak of the walls of Jerusalem. Some scholars think they were added in the time of the exile, since David did not need to pray for the walls. The WABV text keeps them in place.
- **51:19** "Right sacrifices" translates *zivchei tsedeq*, "sacrifices of righteousness".
- **52** The Hebrew heading is verses 1 and 2. The story of Doeg is in 1 Samuel 21:7 and 22:9-19.
- **52:1** "You mighty man" is *gibbor*. "The steadfast love of God lasts all day long" translates *chesed El kol ha-yom*. See entry H-HESED. The line is hard in the Hebrew, and some read it as "against the faithful of God".
- **52:2** "Like a sharp razor" refers to the cutting tongue.
- **52:3** "Speaking what is right" translates *medabber tsedeq*.
- **52:4** "Words that devour" translates *divrei vala*.
- **52:5** "God will break you down for ever" is a prayer or a declaration. The verbs are "break down", "snatch", "tear" and "uproot". "The land of the living" is the world of the living.
- **52:6** "The righteous will see and fear, and will laugh at him" is rendered as written.
- **52:7** "Sought refuge in his own destruction" translates *ya'oz be-havvato*, which may also be read "was strong in his wealth" or "in his ruin". The Greek has "in his vanity".
- **52:8** "A green olive tree in the house of God" is a figure of life in the temple. "The steadfast love of God" is *chesed Elohim*.
- **52:9** "Because you have done it" refers to God's act of rescue. "Your faithful ones" is *chasidekha*. See entry H-HESED.
- **53** "According to Mahalath" (*al machalat*) names a tune, perhaps meaning "sickness" or "dancing". The psalm is nearly the same as Psalm 14, and the WABV text keeps the wording matched where the Hebrew is the same, with "God" for the divine name that is *YHWH* in Psalm 14. The Hebrew differs in 53:1 ("vile injustice", *avel*, for "vile deeds", *alilah*, in 14:1), in 53:3 (where the Hebrew has the rare verb *sag*, "fallen away", for *sar*, "turned aside"; the WABV text keeps "turned aside" in both), and in 53:5, which is a different verse.
- **53:4** "All these evildoers" translates *pe'olei aven*. "Do not call on God" has *Elohim* where Psalm 14:4 has "the LORD".
- **53:5** "Where there was no terror" translates *lo hayah fachad*. "God scatters the bones of the one who camps against you" translates *Elohim pizzar atsmot chonakh*. The verse is hard in the Hebrew, and it takes the place of 14:5-6.
- **53:6** "God restores the fortunes of his people" has *Elohim* where Psalm 14:7 has "the LORD".
- **54** The Hebrew heading is verses 1 and 2. The story of the Ziphites is in 1 Samuel 23:19 and 26:1.
- **54:1** "Save me by your name" is *be-shimkha hoshi'eni*. "By your might" translates *be-gevuratekha*.
- **54:3** "Strangers" follows the Hebrew *zarim*. Some manuscripts have *zedim*, "the arrogant" (see 86:14). "Ruthless men" translates *aritsim*.
- **54:4** "The Lord is the upholder of my life" translates *Adonai be-somekhei nafshi*, which may also be read "the Lord is among those who uphold my life".
- **54:5** "He will return the evil to my enemies" follows the reading tradition (*yashiv*). The prayer "put an end to them" is rendered as written. "In your faithfulness" is *be-amittekha*.
- **54:6** "A freewill offering" is *nedavah*. "Your name, LORD" is the only place in the psalm where *YHWH* is used.
- **54:7** "My eye has looked in triumph on my enemies" says that the poet has seen their defeat, or is certain that he will.
- **55:1** "My plea for mercy" translates *techinnati*.
- **55:2** "Restless" translates *arid*, which may also mean "I wander".
- **55:3** "They bear a grudge against me" translates *yistemuni*.
- **55:6** "Oh, that I had wings like a dove" is the wish of the poet to escape. "Dove" is *yonah*.
- **55:8** "A shelter" is *miflat*. "The raging wind" translates *ruach so'ah*.
- **55:9** "Divide their tongues" recalls the confusion of tongues at Babel (Genesis 11:7-9).
- **55:10** "Day and night they go around it on its walls" refers to violence in the city.
- **55:12** The verses 12 to 14 speak of a friend who betrayed the poet. "My equal" translates *ke-erki*, "one of my rank". "My familiar friend" is *meyudda'i*, "one I know".
- **55:14** "Within God's house we walked in the throng" says that they went to the temple together.
- **55:15** "Let death come upon them" translates a hard verb (*yashsha*), which may be read "let him deceive them" or "let desolation come". "Let them go down to Sheol alive" is rendered as written and recalls Numbers 16:30-33. See entry H-SHEOL.
- **55:17** "Evening and morning and at noon" refers to three times of daily prayer (see Daniel 6:10).
- **55:18** "He redeems my soul in safety" uses *nefesh*. See entry H-NEPHESH.
- **55:19** "They do not change" translates *chaliphot*, a rare word that may mean "changes", "pledges" or "reverses". "Enthroned from of old" is *yoshev qedem*.
- **55:20** "His covenant" is *beritho*. See entry H-BERIT. The verb is *chillel*, "profaned".
- **55:22** "Cast your burden on the LORD" translates *hashlekh al-YHWH yehavkha*, in which *yehav* is a rare word for what is given or laid on a person. The line is quoted in 1 Peter 5:7.
- **55:23** "The pit of destruction" translates *be'er shachat*. "Men of blood and treachery" translates *anshei damim u-mirmah*. "Will not live out half their days" says that they will die young.
- **56** "According to The Dove on Far-off Terebinths" (*al yonat elem rechoqim*) names a tune. "A miktam" is explained at Psalm 16. The story of David's capture at Gath is in 1 Samuel 21:10-15.
- **56:1** "Trample on me" translates *sha'afani*. "An attacker" is *lochem*.
- **56:4** "Whose word I praise" translates *adabber devaro*, which is hard and may mean "I praise his word". "What can flesh do to me?" is repeated in verse 11 and is like 118:6.
- **56:5** "They twist my words" translates *yeatstsevu*, which may also mean "they hurt".
- **56:7** "For their crime will they escape?" translates *al-aven palet-lamo*, which is hard and may be read "on account of iniquity, is there escape for them?"
- **56:8** "You have kept count of my tossings" translates *nodi safarta attah*, in which *nod* means "wandering" or "tossing". "Your bottle" translates *no'dekha*, a skin bottle, a play on the sound of *nod*.
- **56:9** "God is for me" translates *Elohim li*.
- **56:10** "In the LORD, whose word I praise" uses *YHWH*.
- **56:12** "Thank offerings" translates *todot*.
- **56:13** "My soul" is *nafshi*. See entry H-NEPHESH. "The light of life" is *or ha-chayyim*.
- **57** "According to Do Not Destroy" (*al tashchet*) names a tune, and it is used in the headings of Psalms 57, 58, 59 and 75. The cave is the cave of Adullam or of En-gedi (see 1 Samuel 22:1 and 24:3). The verses 7 to 11 are the same as 108:1-5, and the wording of the two must be kept matched when Psalm 108 is drafted.
- **57:1** "My soul" is *nafshi*. See entry H-NEPHESH. "The storms of destruction" translates *havvot*. "The shadow of your wings" is as in 17:8.
- **57:2** "Who fulfils his purpose for me" translates *ha-gomer alai*, "who completes for me".
- **57:3** "God will send out his steadfast love and his faithfulness" shows the two as messengers. "His steadfast love" is *chasdo*. See entry H-HESED. "Selah" falls in the middle of the verse in the Hebrew.
- **57:4** "Fiery beasts" translates *lohatim*, which may mean "devouring" or "flaming". The line speaks of people whose words are weapons.
- **57:5** "Be exalted, God, above the heavens" is a refrain, which is repeated in verse 11.
- **57:8** "My glory" translates *kevodi*, which may mean "my soul" (see 16:9). "I will wake the dawn" translates *a'irah shachar*, the poet's song will wake the morning.
- **57:10** "Your steadfast love is great to the heavens" is as in 36:5.
- **58** "You gods" translates *elem*, which is the form in the Hebrew text. It may mean "in silence", "gods" (*elim*), or "rulers", so that the line may read "Do you indeed speak what is right, you silent ones?" or "you rulers". The WABV text takes "gods", the members of a heavenly court, who have been unjust (see 82:1-8). The psalm is a prayer against unjust judges.
- **58:4** "The deaf cobra" translates *peten*, a snake.
- **58:5** "Charmers" translates *melachashim*, snake charmers.
- **58:6** "God, break the teeth in their mouths" is a prayer against the wicked and is rendered as written.
- **58:7** "When he aims his arrows, let them be blunted" translates *yidrokh chitsav ke-mo yitmolalu*, which is hard and may be read "when he aims his arrows, let them be as if cut off".
- **58:8** "The snail that dissolves into slime" translates *shabbelul temes*. "A stillborn child" translates *nefel*.
- **58:9** "Before your pots can feel the heat of thorns, green or burning, may he sweep them away" is very hard in the Hebrew (*be-terem yavinu sirotekhem atad ke-mo-chai ke-mo-charon yis'arennu*). The sense is uncertain, and it appears to say that the wicked will be removed swiftly.
- **58:10** "The righteous will rejoice when they see the vengeance. They will bathe their feet in the blood of the wicked" is rendered as written, with no softening. It is among the hardest lines in the book. The picture is of the victory of God over the wicked (see Isaiah 63:1-6 and Revelation 14:19-20).
- **58:11** "There is a God who judges on earth" translates *yesh Elohim shofetim ba-arets*, in which the verb is plural, so that the line may be read "there are gods who judge on earth". The WABV text takes the singular.
- **59** The story of Saul's men watching David's house is in 1 Samuel 19:11-17.
- **59:3** "Fierce men" translates *azzim*.
- **59:4** "Come to meet me, and see" is a call to God.
- **59:5** "Show no mercy to any who treacherously plot evil" translates *al-tachon kol-bogedei aven*. "Punish all the nations" is rendered as written. The prayer is against the nations as enemies of the poet and of God.
- **59:6** "Howling like dogs" is repeated in verse 14 and is a refrain.
- **59:7** "With swords on their lips" is a figure of words that cut. "Who hears?" is the thought of the wicked.
- **59:8** "The LORD laughs at them" is like 2:4.
- **59:9** "My Strength, I will watch for you" follows the Greek, the Syriac and some manuscripts (*uzzi*), which are matched by the same word in verse 17. The Hebrew text most often read has "his strength" (*uzzo*). The WABV text departs from the Hebrew reading and is flagged for the scholar.
- **59:10** "My God in his steadfast love will meet me" follows the reading tradition (*Elohai chasdo yeqadmeni*). The written text has "the God of my steadfast love". See entry H-HESED.
- **59:11** "Do not kill them, or my people may forget" is the poet's prayer that the wicked be left to be an example. "Make them totter" translates *haniy'emo*.
- **59:13** "Consume them in wrath. Consume them until they are no more" is rendered as written, with no softening.
- **59:17** "The God who shows me steadfast love" translates *Elohei chasdi*, "my God of steadfast love".
- **60** In Psalm 60 the Hebrew heading is verses 1 and 2, so that English 60:1-12 is Hebrew 60:3-14. In Psalms 61, 62, 63, 64, 65, 67, 68, 69 and 70 the heading is verse 1, so that English 61:1-8 is Hebrew 61:2-9, English 62:1-12 is Hebrew 62:2-13, English 63:1-11 is Hebrew 63:2-12, English 64:1-10 is Hebrew 64:2-11, English 65:1-13 is Hebrew 65:2-14, English 67:1-7 is Hebrew 67:2-8, English 68:1-35 is Hebrew 68:2-36, English 69:1-36 is Hebrew 69:2-37, and English 70:1-5 is Hebrew 70:2-6. Psalms 66, 71 and 72 are numbered alike. In the Greek and Latin numbering these psalms are one lower, from 59 to 71. "Shushan Eduth" means "lily of the testimony" and names a tune. "For instruction" translates *le-lammed*, "to teach". The heading refers to David's wars (see 2 Samuel 8 and 10 and 1 Chronicles 18). It says that Joab struck down twelve thousand of Edom, where 2 Samuel 8:13 says that David struck down eighteen thousand and 1 Chronicles 18:12 says that Abishai did. The numbers come from different accounts.
- **60:1** "You have rejected us" translates *zanachtanu*. "Broken our defences" translates *peratstanu*, "burst upon us".
- **60:2** "You have made the land quake. You have torn it open" is a figure of defeat as an earthquake.
- **60:3** "Wine to drink that made us stagger" translates *yein tarelah*, "wine of staggering".
- **60:4** "A banner" is Hebrew *nes*. "That they may flee to it from the bow" translates *le-hitnosses mi-penei qoshet*, in which *qoshet* may mean "bow" or, as in Aramaic, "truth", so that the line may read "that it may be raised because of truth".
- **60:5** "Your beloved ones" translates *yedidekha*.
- **60:6** "In his holiness" translates *be-qodsho*, which may also be read "in his sanctuary". See entry H-QADOSH. The verses 6 to 8 give an oracle, in which God claims the land of Israel and its neighbours.
- **60:7** "Ephraim is my helmet. Judah is my sceptre" translates *ma'oz roshi* and *mechoqeqi*. "Sceptre" may also be "lawgiver".
- **60:8** "Moab is my washbasin" translates *Mo'av sir rachtsi*. "On Edom I cast my sandal" refers to a claim of ownership (see Ruth 4:7). "Over Philistia I shout in triumph" follows Psalm 108:9. The Hebrew of Psalm 60:8 has "Philistia, shout because of me".
- **60:9** "The fortified city" may be Sela (Petra) in Edom.
- **60:10** "You do not go out, God, with our armies" is the complaint of the people.
- **61:2** "The end of the earth" refers to distance from the temple. "The rock that is higher than I" translates *tsur yarum mimmenni*.
- **61:3** "A strong tower" translates *migdal oz*.
- **61:4** "Your tent" refers to the sanctuary. "The shelter of your wings" is as in 17:8.
- **61:6** The verses 6 and 7 are a prayer for the king. It is not clear whether the poet is the king or speaks for him.
- **61:7** "Appoint steadfast love and faithfulness to watch over him" translates *chesed ve-emet man yintsiruhu*. See entry H-HESED.
- **62** "Jeduthun" is explained at Psalm 39. The refrain "For God alone my soul waits in silence" is repeated in verses 1 and 5.
- **62:1** "For God alone" translates *ak le-Elohim*, in which *ak* may mean "only", "truly" or "yet". "My soul" is *nafshi*. See entry H-NEPHESH. "Waits in silence" translates *dumiyyah*.
- **62:3** "How long will all of you attack a man" translates *ad-anah tehotetu*, in which the verb is rare and may mean "assault" or "shout at". "A leaning wall, a tottering fence" is a figure of the victim.
- **62:4** "They bless with their mouths, but inwardly they curse" says that they flatter.
- **62:8** "Pour out your heart before him" translates *shifkhu lefanav levavkhem*.
- **62:9** "A breath" is *hevel*. "A lie" translates *kazav*.
- **62:10** "Extortion" is *osheq* and "robbery" is *gazel*.
- **62:11** "Once God has spoken. Twice have I heard this" is a numerical form, a way of saying that the poet has heard it many times.
- **62:12** "To you, Lord, belongs steadfast love" translates *ve-lekha Adonai chased*. See entry H-HESED. "You repay a person according to his work" is quoted in Romans 2:6.
- **63** The wilderness of Judah is the place of David's flights (see 1 Samuel 23 to 24 and 2 Samuel 15 to 16).
- **63:1** "I seek you" translates *ashacharekha*, which may also be read "I seek you early". "My soul" is *nafshi*. See entry H-NEPHESH. "My flesh faints for you" translates the rare verb *kamah*.
- **63:2** "So I have looked on you in the sanctuary" says that the poet has seen God in the temple, and wishes to see him again.
- **63:3** "Your steadfast love is better than life" translates *tov chasdekha me-chayyim*. See entry H-HESED.
- **63:5** "Fat and rich food" is the best of a sacrificial meal.
- **63:8** "My soul clings to you" translates *davqah nafshi acharekha*.
- **63:9** "Will go down into the depths of the earth" is Sheol.
- **63:10** "They will be given over to the power of the sword" translates *yaggiruhu al-yedei-charev*, in which the verb means "pour out" or "hand over". "A portion for jackals" says that their bodies will be eaten. The lines are rendered as written.
- **63:11** "The king will rejoice in God. All who swear by him will exult" refers to those who take oaths in God's name.
- **64:1** "In my complaint" translates *be-sichi*, "in my musing" or "my lament". "Dread of the enemy" translates *pachad oyev*.
- **64:2** "The secret plots" translates *sod* and "the throng" is *rigshat*.
- **64:3** "Who sharpen their tongues like swords, who aim bitter words like arrows" is a figure of slander as a weapon.
- **64:4** "The blameless" translates *tam*.
- **64:6** "They search out injustice" translates *yachpesu olot*. "A perfect plot" translates *chefes mechuppas*, literally "a searched-out search", which is hard and may be read "a thoroughly searched plan". "The inward mind and heart of a person are deep" says that people can hide their purposes.
- **64:7** "God shoots his arrow at them" turns the enemies' weapon against them. "They are wounded suddenly" translates *makkat pit'om hayu makkotam*.
- **64:8** "They are brought to ruin, their own tongues turned against them" translates *va-yakhshilu alemo leshonam*, which is hard and may be read "he makes them stumble over their tongue". "Shake their heads" translates *yitnoddu*, a gesture of mockery or horror.
- **64:9** "All people will fear" translates *va-yir'u kol-adam*.
- **65:1** "Praise is due to you" translates *lekha dumiyyah tehillah*, literally "to you silence is praise". It may mean "praise awaits you" or "silence before you is praise".
- **65:2** "All flesh" is *kol basar*, all living creatures.
- **65:3** "When iniquities prevail against me" translates *divrei avonot gavru mennni*, literally "words of iniquities are too strong for me". "You atone for our transgressions" translates *pesha'enu attah tekhapperem*. See entry H-KAPPER.
- **65:4** "Blessed is the one you choose and bring near" is the language of the temple. "Your holy temple" translates *heikhal qodshekha*. See entry H-QADOSH.
- **65:5** "Awesome deeds" translates *nora'ot*. "With righteousness" is *be-tsedeq*. See entry H-TSEDAQAH.
- **65:7** "Who stills the roaring of the seas, the roaring of their waves, the tumult of the peoples" joins the stilling of the sea with the calming of the nations (see Mark 4:39).
- **65:8** "The gateways of the morning and the evening" translates *motsa'ei boqer va-erev*, "the outgoings of the morning and the evening", the east and the west.
- **65:9** "You visit the earth" translates *paqadta ha-arets*. "You greatly enrich it" translates the rare verb *teshoqeqeha*. "The river of God" translates *pelev Elohim*, the stream of rain. "For so you have prepared it" translates *ki-khen tekhinah*, which is hard.
- **65:11** "Your wagon tracks overflow with abundance" translates *ma'galeikha yir'afun deshen*, literally "your paths drip with fatness".
- **66:3** "Your enemies come cringing to you" translates *yekhachashu lekha oyeveikha*. The same verb is in 18:44.
- **66:6** "He turned the sea into dry land. They passed through the river on foot" refers to the Red Sea (Exodus 14) and the Jordan (Joshua 3).
- **66:7** "The rebellious" translates *sorerim*.
- **66:9** "Who has kept our soul among the living" translates *ha-sam nafshenu ba-chayyim*.
- **66:10** "You have refined us as silver is refined" is a figure of testing by fire.
- **66:11** "A crushing burden" translates *mu'aqah*, a rare word.
- **66:12** "You let people ride over our heads" is a figure of defeat. "A place of abundance" translates *revayah*, which means "saturation" or "overflowing" and is hard.
- **66:15** "Fattened animals" translates *mechim*.
- **66:16** "What he has done for my soul" uses *nafshi*. See entry H-NEPHESH.
- **66:18** "If I had cherished iniquity in my heart, the Lord would not have listened" translates *im-aven ra'iti be-libbi*, literally "if I had seen iniquity in my heart", which may mean "looked on with favour" (see Job 27:9, Proverbs 28:9 and Isaiah 1:15).
- **66:20** "His steadfast love" is *chasdo*. See entry H-HESED.
- **67:1** "May God be gracious to us and bless us and make his face shine on us" recalls the priestly blessing (Numbers 6:24-26).
- **67:2** "Your saving power" translates *yeshu'atekha*.
- **67:4** "With equity" is *mishor*. "Guide" translates *tanchem*.
- **67:6** "The earth has yielded its increase" translates *erets natena yevulah*. The verb is in the past tense, but the line may also be read as a wish ("let the earth yield").
- **68** Psalm 68 is the hardest psalm of the book in the Hebrew, and the text may be damaged in many verses. The footnotes record only the main difficulties.
- **68:1** "Let God arise. Let his enemies be scattered" is the old song of the ark when it set out (see Numbers 10:35).
- **68:2** "As smoke is driven away" and "as wax melts before fire" are figures of the end of the wicked.
- **68:4** "Who rides through the deserts" translates *rokhev ba-aravot*, in which *aravot* may mean "deserts" or "clouds" (see verse 33 and the old songs of Canaan, in which the storm god rides on the clouds). "Yah" is the short form of the name *YHWH* (see Exodus 15:2 and Isaiah 12:2). It is the form in "Hallelujah".
- **68:5** "Protector of widows" translates *dayyan almanot*, literally "judge of widows". "His holy dwelling" translates *me'on qodsho*. See entry H-QADOSH.
- **68:6** "The solitary" translates *yechidim*, those who are alone. "To prosperity" translates the rare word *ba-kosharot*, which may also mean "with singing".
- **68:7** The verses 7 and 8 recall the march in the wilderness and the appearance at Sinai (see Judges 5:4-5).
- **68:8** "The One of Sinai" translates *zeh Sinai*, "this Sinai", a phrase that may be an old title of God.
- **68:9** "You shed abundant rain" translates *geshem nedavot*, "a rain of generosity". "When it languished" translates *ve-nil'ah*.
- **68:10** "Your flock found a dwelling in it" translates *chayyatekha yashvu bah*, in which *chayyah* may mean "flock", "band" or "living creatures".
- **68:11** The verses 11 to 14 are very hard in the Hebrew. "The women who announce the news" translates *ha-mevasserot*, the women who bring the news of victory (see Exodus 15:20 and 1 Samuel 18:6).
- **68:12** "She who stays at home divides the spoil" translates *nevat bayit techalleq shalal*.
- **68:13** "Though you lie among the sheepfolds" translates *im-tishkevun ben shefatayim*, in which *shefatayim* is a rare word that may mean "sheepfolds", "saddlebags" or "ash heaps". "The wings of a dove covered with silver" is hard and may refer to the spoil.
- **68:14** "It was like snow falling on Zalmon" translates *be-farsas Shaddai melakhim bah tashleg be-Tsalmon*. "Zalmon" is a mountain (see Judges 9:48), and the figure may mean the scattering of the kings like snow or the whiteness of the bones.
- **68:15** "The mountain of Bashan" is Mount Hermon or the high country of Bashan. "Many-peaked" translates the rare word *gavnunnim*.
- **68:16** "Why do you look with envy" translates *lammah teratsedun*, a rare verb. The mountains of Bashan are shown as envying Zion.
- **68:17** "Twice ten thousand, thousands upon thousands" translates *ribbotayim alfei shin'an*, in which *shin'an* is a rare word. "Sinai is now in the sanctuary" translates *Sinai ba-qodesh*, which is hard. It says that the presence of God from Sinai is now in the temple.
- **68:18** "You ascended on high" refers to the ascent of God to Zion. "Receiving gifts from people" follows the Hebrew *laqachta matanot ba-adam*. The Greek and the Aramaic have "gave gifts to people", which is quoted in Ephesians 4:8. The WABV text follows the Hebrew.
- **68:19** "Who bears us up day by day" translates *yom yom ya'amos lanu*, "who carries our load".
- **68:20** "The ways out of death" translates *totsa'ot la-mavet*, "the escapes from death".
- **68:21** "The hairy crown" translates *qodqod se'ar*, "the hairy scalp".
- **68:23** "That you may bathe your feet in blood" translates *timchats raglekha be-dam*, in which *timchats* may mean "strike" or "dip". The picture is the same as in 58:10, and the line is rendered as written. "The tongues of your dogs" is a figure of dogs that eat the dead.
- **68:24** "Your procession" translates *halikhotekha*, the entry of God into the sanctuary.
- **68:25** "Young women playing tambourines" translates *alamot tofefot*. See Exodus 15:20.
- **68:26** "You of the fountain of Israel" translates *mi-meqor Yisra'el*, "you from the source of Israel".
- **68:27** "Benjamin, the youngest" translates *Binyamin tsa'ir*, the smallest of the tribes. "Leading them" translates *rodem*, which may also mean "ruling them". "Their throng" translates *rigmatam*, a rare word. The four tribes are two from the south and two from the north.
- **68:28** "Summon your power, God" follows the Greek and the Syriac, in which the verb is a command. The Hebrew text has "Your God has commanded your strength" (*tsivvah Elohekha uzzekha*). The WABV text departs from the Hebrew reading and is flagged for the scholar.
- **68:29** "Because of your temple at Jerusalem" is hard in the Hebrew.
- **68:30** "The beast of the reeds" translates *chayyat qaneh*, a figure of Egypt, perhaps the crocodile or the hippopotamus. "Trample those who lust after tribute" translates *mitrapes be-ratsei-kesef*, which is hard and may be read "trampling on bars of silver".
- **68:31** "Envoys" translates *chashmannim*, a rare word that may mean "envoys", "nobles" or "bronze goods". "Cush will hasten to stretch out her hands to God" translates *Kush tarits yadav le-Elohim*.
- **68:33** "The ancient heavens" translates *shemei-qedem*. "His mighty voice" is *qol oz*.
- **69** "According to Lilies" is explained at Psalm 45.
- **69:1** "To my neck" translates *ad-nafesh*, literally "to the soul". The word *nefesh* may mean "throat", "neck" or "life". See entry H-NEPHESH.
- **69:3** "My throat is parched" translates *nachar geroni*.
- **69:4** "Without cause" is *chinnam*. The line is quoted in John 15:25. "What I did not steal I must now restore" is a saying of one who is made to pay for what he did not take.
- **69:5** "You know my folly" translates *attah yada'ta le-ivvalti*. The poet admits some fault, though he says in verse 7 that his reproach is for God's sake.
- **69:9** "Zeal for your house has consumed me" is quoted in John 2:17, and "the reproaches of those who reproach you have fallen on me" in Romans 15:3.
- **69:10** "My soul" is *nafshi*. See entry H-NEPHESH.
- **69:12** "Those who sit in the gate" are the elders and judges of the town.
- **69:13** "At an acceptable time" translates *et ratson*, "a time of favour". "Your steadfast love" is *chasdekha*. See entry H-HESED. "Your saving faithfulness" translates *be-emet yish'ekha*.
- **69:15** "The pit close its mouth over me" translates *ve-al-te'tar alai be'er pi-ha*.
- **69:18** "Draw near to my soul and redeem it" translates *qorvah el-nafshi ge'alah*. The verb *ga'al* is the verb of the redeemer (see entry H-GOEL).
- **69:21** "Poison" translates *rosh*, a bitter plant. "Sour wine" translates *chometz*. The verse is echoed in Matthew 27:34, 48 and John 19:29.
- **69:22** The verses 22 to 28 are a prayer against the enemies. They are rendered as written, with no softening. Verses 22 and 23 are quoted in Romans 11:9-10, and verse 25 in Acts 1:20.
- **69:27** "May they have no acquittal from you" translates *al-yavo'u be-tsidqatekha*, literally "let them not come into your righteousness". See entry H-TSEDAQAH.
- **69:28** "The book of the living" recalls Exodus 32:32-33.
- **69:29** "But I am afflicted and in pain" begins the turn to praise.
- **69:33** "His own people who are prisoners" translates *asirav*, "his prisoners".
- **69:35** The last verses (35 and 36) speak of the rebuilding of the cities of Judah. Some scholars think that they were added in the time of the exile. The WABV text keeps them in place.
- **70** "For the memorial offering" is explained at Psalm 38. The psalm is nearly the same as Psalm 40:13-17, and the WABV text keeps the wording matched where the Hebrew is the same. The Hebrew differs in 70:1 ("God" and "LORD" in the order of the two names), in 70:2 (where the words "snatch away" are not there), in 70:3 ("turn back because of their shame", *yashuvu*, for "be appalled", *yashommu*, in 40:15), in 70:4 ("God is great", with *Elohim*, where 40:16 has "Great is the LORD"), and in 70:5 (where the last line is "LORD, do not delay" and not "the Lord takes thought for me" and "my God" as in 40:17).
- **71** Psalm 71 has no heading in the Hebrew. The Greek calls it a psalm of David, of the sons of Jonadab and the first who were taken captive. Its first verses (1 to 3) are like 31:1-3.
- **71:2** "In your righteousness" translates *be-tsidqatekha*. See entry H-TSEDAQAH.
- **71:3** "A rock of refuge, to which I may always come" follows the reading *tsur ma'on la-vo tamid*, in which *ma'on* means "dwelling". Some manuscripts have *ma'oz*, "stronghold", as in 31:2. "You have given the command to save me" translates *tsivvita le-hoshi'eni*.
- **71:6** "It was you who took me from my mother's womb" translates *attah gozi mi-me'ei immi*, in which *gozi* is a rare word that may mean "brought me out" or "cut me" (see 22:9).
- **71:7** "A portent to many" translates *ke-mofet le-rabbim*. It may mean a sign of wonder or of warning.
- **71:9** "Old age" translates *zikhnah*.
- **71:13** "My accusers" translates *sotenei nafshi*, from the verb *satan*, "to accuse" (see entry H-SATAN).
- **71:15** "Your righteous acts" translates *tsidqotekha*. See entry H-TSEDAQAH. "I do not know their number" translates *lo yada'ti sefarot*, in which *sefarot* is a rare word that may mean "numbers" or "accounts".
- **71:16** "The mighty deeds of the Lord GOD" translates *gevurot Adonai YHWH*.
- **71:18** "Old age and grey hairs" translates *zikhnah ve-seivah*. "Another generation" is the next generation.
- **71:20** "You who have made me see many troubles and calamities will revive me again" follows the reading tradition, in which the verbs are singular ("me"). The written text has "us". "From the depths of the earth" translates *mi-tehomot ha-arets*, a figure of the grave, or of great danger.
- **71:22** "The Holy One of Israel" translates *qedosh Yisra'el*, a title that is used often in Isaiah. See entry H-QADOSH.
- **71:23** "My soul" is *nafshi*. See entry H-NEPHESH.
- **72** "Of Solomon" translates *li-Shelomoh*, which may mean "by", "for" or "about" Solomon. The psalm is a prayer for the king.
- **72:1** "Your justice" translates *mishpatekha*, and "your righteousness" is *tsidqatekha*. See entry H-TSEDAQAH. "The royal son" translates *ben-melekh*, "the king's son".
- **72:3** "Peace" is *shalom*. See entry H-SHALOM.
- **72:4** "The poor of the people" translates *aniyyei am*, and "crush the oppressor" is *yedakke oshek*.
- **72:5** "May they fear you while the sun endures" follows the Hebrew *yira'ukha*. The Greek has "and he will continue with the sun" (*ya'arikh*).
- **72:6** "The mown grass" translates *gez*, grass cut for hay.
- **72:8** "From sea to sea and from the River to the ends of the earth" is quoted in Zechariah 9:10. "The River" is the Euphrates.
- **72:9** "Desert tribes" translates *tsiyyim*, which may also be read "his adversaries" (*tsarav*).
- **72:10** "Tarshish" and "the coastlands" are far to the west. "Sheba and Seba" are far to the south (see 1 Kings 10:1-13).
- **72:14** "Their blood is precious in his sight" says that their lives are valued.
- **72:15** "Long may he live" translates *ve-yechi*. "Gold of Sheba" is a gift of the south.
- **72:16** "Abundance of grain" translates *pissat-bar*, in which *pissah* is a rare word that may mean "handful" or "abundance". "May it wave" translates *yir'ash*, "may it shake".
- **72:17** "May people be blessed in him" translates *ve-yitbarekhu vo*, which may also be read "may they bless themselves by him" (see Genesis 12:3 and 22:18).
- **72:18** "Blessed be the LORD, the God of Israel, who alone does wonderful things" is the closing blessing of Book Two (see also 41:13, 89:52 and 106:48).
- **72:20** "The prayers of David, the son of Jesse, are ended" is a note at the end of the early collection of the psalms of David. It does not mean that no psalm after this is of David, since later psalms also bear his name. The word "prayers" translates *tefillot*.
- **73** Book Three (Psalms 73 to 89) begins here. In Psalms 73, 74, 78, 79 and 82 the heading is part of verse 1, or there is no separate heading verse, and the numbering is the same in Hebrew and English. In Psalms 75, 76, 77, 80, 81 and 83 the heading is verse 1, so that English 75:1-10 is Hebrew 75:2-11, English 76:1-12 is Hebrew 76:2-13, English 77:1-20 is Hebrew 77:2-21, English 80:1-19 is Hebrew 80:2-20, English 81:1-16 is Hebrew 81:2-17, and English 83:1-18 is Hebrew 83:2-19. In the Greek and Latin numbering these psalms are one lower, from 72 to 82.
- **73:1** "Truly God is good to Israel" translates *ak tov le-Yisra'el Elohim*, in which *ak* may mean "truly", "only" or "yet". "Pure in heart" translates *bare levav*.
- **73:3** "The arrogant" translates *holelim*. "The prosperity of the wicked" translates *shalom resha'im*, "the peace of the wicked".
- **73:4** "They have no pangs until death" translates *ein charzubbot le-motam*, which is hard and may be read "there are no pangs in their death". "Fat and sleek" translates *u-vari ulam*, which is also hard.
- **73:6** "Pride is their necklace. Violence covers them as a garment" is a figure of the wicked wearing their sins as ornaments.
- **73:7** "Their eyes swell out with fatness" translates *yatsa me-chelev eimo*. "Their hearts overflow with follies" translates *ya'avru maskiyyot levav*.
- **73:9** "They set their mouths against the heavens" says that they speak against God.
- **73:10** "Therefore his people turn back to them and find no fault in them" is very hard in the Hebrew. It may say that even the people of God follow the wicked and drink their water in abundance.
- **73:11** "How can God know?" is the thought of the wicked (see 10:11 and 94:7).
- **73:13** "Washed my hands in innocence" is as in 26:6.
- **73:15** "I would have betrayed the generation of your children" says that the poet would have led others astray.
- **73:17** "The sanctuary of God" translates *miqdeshei El*, a plural of majesty. "Their end" translates *acharitam*.
- **73:20** "Phantoms" translates *tsalmam*, "their image", which may be read "their shadows".
- **73:21** "Pricked in my inward parts" translates *kilyotai*, "my kidneys".
- **73:22** "Like a beast toward you" translates *behemot hayiti immakh*, "I was beasts with you".
- **73:24** "Afterward you will receive me with honour" translates *ve-achar kavod tiqqacheni*, in which *kavod* may mean "honour", "glory" or be joined to "afterward" ("after glory"). The verb *laqach*, "receive" or "take", is the verb used of Enoch (Genesis 5:24) and Elijah (2 Kings 2:10), and see 49:15. The poet may hope for rescue in this life or for life with God beyond death. The Hebrew does not say which.
- **73:26** "God is the strength of my heart and my portion for ever" translates *tsur levavi ve-cheleqi Elohim le-olam*. The word *tsur* is "rock". The "portion" is the language of the Levites (see 16:5).
- **73:27** "Everyone who is unfaithful to you" translates *kol zoneh mimmekha*, literally "everyone who is a prostitute from you".
- **74** "A maskil" is explained at Psalm 32. The psalm is a lament over the burning of the temple. The date is not certain. It may be the destruction of Jerusalem in 587 BC or a later attack.
- **74:1** "Cast us off for ever" translates *zanachta la-netsach*. "The sheep of your pasture" is a figure of the people.
- **74:2** "Your congregation" translates *adatekha*. "Which you redeemed to be the tribe of your inheritance" recalls Exodus 15:13, 16.
- **74:3** "Direct your steps" translates *harimah pe'amekha*, "lift up your steps". "The perpetual ruins" translates *mashu'ot netsach*.
- **74:4** "Your meeting place" translates *mo'edekha*. "They set up their own signs as signs" refers to the standards of the enemy.
- **74:5** The verses 5 and 6 are hard in the Hebrew, and the words for the tools (*kashil* and *keilappot*) are rare.
- **74:7** "They set your sanctuary on fire" refers to the burning of the temple. "The dwelling place of your name" is the temple.
- **74:8** "The meeting places of God" translates *mo'adei El*, which has been understood as the places of worship through the land, including the synagogues, or as the festivals.
- **74:9** "There is no longer any prophet" says that the voice of prophecy has stopped.
- **74:11** "The fold of your garment" translates *cheqekha*, "your bosom".
- **74:13** "You divided the sea by your might" refers to the Red Sea. "The sea monsters" translates *tanninim*.
- **74:14** "Leviathan" is as in Job 41. "The creatures of the wilderness" translates *le-am le-tsiyyim*, which may mean "for the people of the desert" or "for desert creatures".
- **74:15** "Ever-flowing streams" translates *naharot eitan*, "streams that never fail".
- **74:16** "The heavenly lights and the sun" translates *maor va-shamesh*.
- **74:19** "The soul of your dove" translates *nefesh toreka*, and "the wild beasts" translates *chayyat*, which is hard and may be singular.
- **74:20** "The covenant" is Hebrew *berit*. See entry H-BERIT. "The dark places of the land" translates *machashakkei erets*.
- **74:21** "The oppressed" translates *dak*.
- **74:22** "Defend your cause" translates *rivah rivekha*.
- **74:23** "The uproar" translates *sha'on*.
- **75** "According to Do Not Destroy" names a tune (see Psalm 57).
- **75:1** "Your name is near" says that God is present. "We recount your wonderful deeds" is the response of the people.
- **75:2** The verses 2 to 5 are spoken by God. "The set time" translates *mo'ed*. "With equity" translates *mesharim*.
- **75:3** "When the earth totters ... it is I who keep its pillars steady" refers to the old picture of the earth as built on pillars (see Job 9:6).
- **75:4** "The horn" is a figure of strength and pride.
- **75:5** "A stiff neck" translates *tsavvar atiq*, which is hard and may be read "insolently".
- **75:6** "Lifting up" translates *harim*. The Hebrew has "not from the east or from the west and not from the wilderness lifting", in which the line is incomplete and the word for "south" is missing. The word *harim* may be read "lifting up" or "mountains".
- **75:8** "A cup with foaming wine, well mixed" is the cup of God's anger (see Isaiah 51:17, Jeremiah 25:15 and Revelation 14:10). "Dregs" translates *shemarim*.
- **75:9** "But I will declare it for ever" is the change of speaker from God to the poet.
- **76:2** "Salem" is Jerusalem (see Genesis 14:18).
- **76:3** "The flashing arrows" translates *rishpei qashet*, literally "the flames of the bow".
- **76:4** "Than the mountains full of prey" follows the Hebrew *harerei tarf*. The Greek has "than the eternal mountains", which may be the original reading.
- **76:5** "The stouthearted" translates *abbirei lev*. "Sank into sleep" is a figure of death.
- **76:6** "Lay stunned" translates *nirdam*, "sank into a deep sleep".
- **76:9** "The humble of the earth" translates *anvei erets*.
- **76:10** "Surely the wrath of man will praise you. The remnant of wrath you will put on like a belt" is very hard in the Hebrew (*she'erit chemot tachgor*). It may be read "the remnant of the wrath you gird on", or, with a change of the vowels, "the survivors of Hamath will keep your festival".
- **76:12** "Who cuts off the spirit of princes" translates *yivtsor ruach negidim*, in which the verb is used of cutting the grapes. See entry H-RUACH.
- **77:1** The psalm has "Asaph" and "Jeduthun" in the heading (see Psalm 39).
- **77:2** "My hand is stretched out without wearying" translates *yadi niggerah ve-lo tafug*, which may mean "my hand flows" (with tears) "and does not stop". "My soul" is *nafshi*. See entry H-NEPHESH.
- **77:3** "My spirit faints" translates *titatef ruchi*.
- **77:4** "You hold my eyelids open" translates *achazta shemurot einai*.
- **77:6** "My song in the night" translates *neginati ba-lailah*. "Then my spirit made a diligent search" translates *va-yechapes ruchi*.
- **77:7** The questions in verses 7 to 9 are the heart of the psalm.
- **77:8** "His steadfast love" is *chasdo*. See entry H-HESED. "His promises" translates *omer*, "his word".
- **77:10** "Then I said, 'I will appeal to this, to the years of the right hand of the Most High'" is very hard in the Hebrew (*challoti hi shenot yemin Elyon*). It may be read "this is my grief, that the right hand of the Most High has changed".
- **77:13** "Your way, God, is holy" translates *ba-qodesh darkekha Elohim*, which may also be read "your way, God, is in the sanctuary". See entry H-QADOSH.
- **77:15** "The children of Jacob and Joseph" names the northern and the southern tribes together.
- **77:16** "The waters saw you" refers to the sea at the exodus.
- **77:18** "The whirlwind" translates *galgal*, which may mean "whirlwind" or "wheel".
- **77:19** "Yet your footprints were unseen" says that God's way was hidden even as he led the people.
- **77:20** "By the hand of Moses and Aaron" turns the psalm from the cry of the poet to the history of the people.
- **78** Psalm 78 is the longest of the historical psalms. It tells the story from Egypt to David, in order to teach the next generation. It shows the people's repeated unfaithfulness and God's repeated mercy and anger. Its account differs in some points from the books of Exodus and Numbers, and the footnotes record the matter where it bears on the wording.
- **78:1** "My teaching" translates *torati*. See entry H-TORAH. It is the poet's instruction.
- **78:2** "A parable" is Hebrew *mashal* and "dark sayings" is *chidot*, "riddles". The verse is quoted in Matthew 13:35.
- **78:5** "A testimony" translates *edut*. "A teaching" translates *torah*. See entry H-TORAH.
- **78:8** "Whose spirit was not faithful to God" uses *ruach*. See entry H-RUACH.
- **78:9** "The Ephraimites, armed with the bow, turned back on the day of battle" refers to an event that is not known. The writer may refer to an old defeat of the tribe of Ephraim.
- **78:10** "God's covenant" is Hebrew *berit Elohim*. See entry H-BERIT.
- **78:12** "The fields of Zoan" refers to the region of Tanis in Egypt (see Numbers 13:22 and Isaiah 19:11).
- **78:13** "He divided the sea" refers to Exodus 14. "Like a heap" is as in Exodus 15:8.
- **78:15** "He split rocks in the wilderness" refers to Exodus 17:6 and Numbers 20:11. "As from the deep" translates *ke-tehomot*.
- **78:18** "By demanding the food they craved" refers to Exodus 16 and Numbers 11.
- **78:21** "A fire was kindled against Jacob" refers to Numbers 11:1-3.
- **78:24** "The grain of heaven" is the manna (see Exodus 16:4, 14-15).
- **78:25** "The bread of the mighty" translates *lechem abbirim*, in which *abbirim* means "mighty ones". The Greek has "the bread of angels", which is followed in the book of Wisdom 16:20. The Hebrew may speak of the bread that the heavenly beings eat.
- **78:31** "He killed the strongest of them" refers to Numbers 11:33-34.
- **78:33** "A breath" is *hevel*.
- **78:34** "They turned back" uses *shuv*. See entry H-SHUV.
- **78:35** "God Most High their redeemer" translates *El Elyon go'alam*. See entry H-GOEL.
- **78:38** "Atoned for their iniquity" translates *yekhapper avon*. See entry H-KAPPER.
- **78:39** "A wind that passes and does not return" says that human life does not come back.
- **78:41** "The Holy One of Israel" translates *qedosh Yisra'el*. See entry H-QADOSH. "Provoked" translates the rare verb *hitvu*.
- **78:43** The verses 43 to 51 give the plagues of Egypt in a different order and a different selection from the book of Exodus. They are the blood, the flies, the frogs, the locusts, the hail, the pestilence and the death of the firstborn. See also Psalm 105:26-36.
- **78:44** "He turned their rivers to blood" refers to Exodus 7:14-25.
- **78:45** "Swarms of flies" translates *arov*.
- **78:46** "The destroying locust" translates *chasil* and "the swarming locust" is *arbeh*.
- **78:47** "Their sycamores with frost" translates *shikmotam ba-chanamal*, in which *chanamal* is a rare word that may mean "frost" or "hail".
- **78:48** "Bolts of lightning" translates *reshafim*.
- **78:49** "A company of destroying messengers" translates *mishlachat malakhei ra'im*, "a sending of messengers of evil". The messengers may be angels.
- **78:51** "The firstfruits of their strength" translates *reshit onim*. "The tents of Ham" refers to Egypt (see Psalm 105:23).
- **78:54** "His holy land" translates *gevul qodsho*, "the territory of his holiness". See entry H-QADOSH. "The hill country" is *har*.
- **78:57** "They twisted like a deceitful bow" translates *nehpekhu ke-qeshet remiyyah*, a bow that shoots wide of the mark.
- **78:58** "Their high places" and "their idols" refer to places of worship not at the temple.
- **78:60** "He forsook his dwelling at Shiloh" refers to the loss of the ark (see 1 Samuel 4 and Jeremiah 7:12).
- **78:61** "His power" and "his glory" refer to the ark (see 1 Samuel 4:21-22).
